ParlaMint-DK_20191009130143	Then it's the Secretary of State. Then we go to 1⁄2 minutes. Along the way, we also have a fellow questioner, Mr. Peter Skaarup. But first it's the Secretary of State for two minutes. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20191009130157	Thank you. Thank you, sir. Morten Messerschmidt for the question. I fully agree that it is essential that there is trust between us politicians and the people we represent, but I do not agree that international cooperation undermines people's confidence in democracy, on the contrary. The government fully supports the rule-based international cooperation, which Denmark is fortunately part of. In fact, I believe that the vast majority of Danes agree that we need international rules of play to ensure that large countries cannot just pry small countries; that human rights are respected; that large companies pay their taxes and meet high requirements, for example. environmental protection, food safety, labour rights; that we need international cooperation that promotes common ambitious climate targets; and that we cooperate to resolve the refugee and migration crisis. That is why it is important that we have organisations such as the United Nations, as the European Union, as the Council of Europe. And when we have binding common ground rules, it is equally important that we have international courts that ensure that these rules are actually observed. Having said that, we do not, of course, agree with everything that is decided, but we believe that we must work actively to safeguard Danish interests where decisions are made. This requires that we be at the negotiating table. And we must work for reforms where appropriate. We must always ensure that international cooperation makes sense and that the problems are solved where they make sense. That is how we take best care of Denmark, and it is possible to say on the European Union and the international community. Thank you, President.

This also applies to the savings provided that there is room under the annual spending framework and investment framework or that new funding is indicated. In fact, for savings, public finances are burdened in the same way as if a whole new appropriation were allocated. There is therefore no difference between the use of savings or the allocation of new appropriations, and that is why decisions in the conciliation circle must also be given priority and coordinated in relation to the other transversal fiscal policy, and that is not to repeat myself, but the horizontal financial policy is something decided under the auspices of the Finance Minister.

At the same time, we are engaged in dialogue with the business community on the issues raised, including transparency in the valuation of business transfers, for example. to the next generation. The roll-back guarantees an economic space of 1 billion. kr. Every year, which we want to spend on promoting equal opportunities. And that is why we are in the middle of a new freedom struggle today, and it is a battle that I would very much like to take part in, but not in the way the questioner is proposing here, that is to say, by settling for religion when granting citizenship, and I would also like to try to explain why. For me, this is more a battle between all democrats, including moderate Muslims, who have long since found their way into Western democratic societies, and then those reactionary awakened Muslims who insist that Sharia regulate society more than it is a conflict between Muslims and European societies. We must stand next to the first and fight against the last. We are fighting for democracy and not against Islam, and that is why I would be worried about dealing with religion when granting citizenship. Some groups of people may end up being discriminated against on the basis of their religion, and in this way we are really pushing the wrong people away from us, and this will be a step backwards in the freedom struggle for democratic values, which I think is so crucial not only for Denmark but for the whole of Europe.

ParlaMint-DK_20191009174913	The debate is open and the first to speak is Mrs Annette Lind from the Socialist Group. Welcome.
ParlaMint-DK_20191009174953	Thank you for that, President. Today we are considering B 6, which is a motion for a resolution on Danish military contributions to the UN peacekeeping mission in Mali, also known as MINUSMA, and B 7, which is a motion for a resolution on a military contribution to Operation Barkhane. On 25 April 2013, the United Nations Security Council adopted a resolution on the establishment of a MINUSMA in Mali. Both B 6, the peacekeeping mission, and the Operation Barkhane, which we deal with in B 7, are part of the security package recently presented by the government on military contributions to defence of Danish interests. The operation "MINUSMA" and "Operation Barkhane" are inextricably linked. The UN resolutions, which provide for the mandate of MINUSMA in Mali, also welcome the Operation Barkhane. In the event of an attack, the operation helps Barkhane. The situation in the Sahel region is developing in a negative direction. There is an escalation of violence between ethnic groups, the situation has driven a great many people to flee, and there are many civilian killings. The peace process is fragile and the situation is worrying. The region is characterised by extreme poverty, high population growth, youth unemployment, weak state institutions and difficult climatic conditions. That is why the military contribution in the Sahel region goes hand in hand with a comprehensive Danish development commitment, with stability contributions and humanitarian aid. The contribution, which we take a position on in B6, is actually a transport aircraft contribution of up to 65 people and a staff contribution of up to 10 people to the UN peacekeeping mission MINUSMA. The personal contribution shall be added from January 2020 to the end of 2020 and shall consist of interpreters and IT specialists, who shall resolve information retrieval tasks in order to contribute to the situational picture of the MINUSMA and for the protection of devices. The Danish personal contribution must form part of a European intelligence unit. The transport aircraft is of type C-130J, also called a Hercules aircraft. The transport aircraft shall contribute for 6 months from November 2019 onwards. The aircraft must carry personnel and equipment. Following the outbreak of the conflict in Mali in 2012, Denmark has supported the international efforts in several rounds in line with the UN mandate in MINUSMA and it has often been extended. The Danish contribution has also previously sent out transport aircraft and also Danish special forces, who have been on mission, and last a Danish Major General has been deployed. On the basis of international law for the Danish contributions to the MINUSMA, the UN Security Council has established that the situation in Mali poses a threat to national peace and security. Then to B 7. The two missions, as I said earlier, have a connection with international engagement to stabilise the Sahel region, which has experienced a worsening security policy situation in recent years. Since 2013, the defence has contributed continuously to the military interventions in Mali and the Sahel. The Foreign Minister and the Minister for Defence have consulted the members of Foreign Policy Name and asked for a mandate in B 7 for a helicopter contribution with 2 EH101 helicopters and up to 70 people. In addition, 1-2 staff officers shall be seconded to the head office of the French-led Operation Barkhane in the Sahel. Cyber capacity will also be included in the Danish military effort. The helicopter contribution shall be added from January 2020 and the year out. For social democracy, it is important that we link military contributions with long-term diplomatic, economic and development policy efforts. The Operation Barkhane is a French-led counter-terrorism operation in the Sahel region, which includes Mali, Burkina Faso, Mauritania, Niger and Chad. Extremism, trafficking in human beings and other forms of cross-border organised crime ravage the Sahel region. This operation must ensure that rural areas in the region do not fall into the wrong hands, as they are already a threat to peace and stability in Europe. Many people are fleeing from conflict, poverty and natural disasters, and the Sahel region is a central transit region for migration from West Africa to Europe. Terrorist groups, such as Al-Qaeda and ISIL, increase their activities and military presence, thus also regularly attacking UN peacekeeping mission MINUSMA in Mali, national forces and the civilian population. We cannot look at that implicitly. But the operation is not safe for Danish soldiers. Denmark is a core alliance in NATO, which is why it is important that we increase our share of the burden. Denmark and the Danish defences are helping to contribute to peace and stability in the world's hot spots. For me, it is important that we take an active part in the international fight against terrorism and strife by taking responsibility out in the world. The peacekeeping mission of MINUSMA and the operation here help to support security and stability in the region; it is important to bring about peace in the long term. Denmark plays a key role in a new regional peace and stabilisation programme in the Sahel region under the Peace and Stabilisation Fund. The Social Democratic Party supports the UN Missions MINUSMA and Operation Barkhane and motions for resolutions B 6 and B 7.

Against this background, New Citizens cannot agree to motion for a resolution B6. The same general considerations also apply to motion for a resolution B 7, which is why new citizens cannot agree to this either.

ParlaMint-DK_20191009182830	Thank you, Mr President. First of all, I would like to thank you for the good and constructive debate. If you now take the threat from terrorists in Mali and the Sahel region against Europe and the West, I can inform you that the Defence Intelligence Service considers that in the short to medium term it is very likely that military extremists will plan attacks against Western targets and plan abductions by Westerners in the region, mainly Mali, Burkina Faso and Niger. Radicalisation and terrorism are increasingly gaining ground in the region and thus also pose a threat to the local population and the international efforts that are regularly under attack. The region is a breeding ground for both terrorism and irregular migration. The terrorist threat comes, among other things. from al-Qaeda and ISIL-related networks which finance their activities through cross-border crime, including: smuggling of people, weapons, drugs, and it also ends up in Europe. So I can only agree with the rapporteur that it is in our own interest, including in Denmark, to be at the forefront of this fight. To Mr. Martin Lidegaard: also thanks for the support. And I think it is also important to emphasise that in Denmark we have a broad-spectrum contribution to the whole Sahel region, as Mr. Martin Lidegaard mentioned it. We contribute through a broad and subtle commitment to improving the living conditions of the people; stabilisation of the Sahel in cooperation with national, regional and international parties. We do this through various initiatives, both with an active political commitment in the region and also through the European Union: long-term development cooperation with Mali, some 200 million. kr. A year, Burkina Faso, about 200 million. kr. a year, and Niger, about 100 million. kr. a year. We have a regional peace and stabilisation programme for the Sahel totalling 158 million. kr. in the period 2018-2021, including contributions from Norway. Dispatch of experts to the civilian capacity building missions in the Sahel under the EU's Common Security and Defence Policy, including a Danish sous chief for the mission in Mali, humanitarian assistance to the crisis in Mali and neighbouring countries affected by the crisis, approximately 73 million. kr. in 2018 and until now approximately 62 million. kr. This Regulation shall enter into force on the twentieth day following that of its publication in the Official Journal of the European Union. So Denmark, by virtue of that commitment, has a solid starting point for contributing to international efforts in relation to security and development in Mali. And I am pleased that the rapporteur points out that this is a concerted and wide-ranging effort. We can see the unstable situation, not least in Libya, what Mrs Flyvholm also mentioned, and that remains part of the great challenge facing Europe's southern neighbourhood. And so, I would say, is of direct foreign policy interest to Europe and Denmark. It is important for our security to help stabilise the region. As I mentioned, countries in the Sahel are among the world's poorest: high population growth, high unemployment, moreover, also increasing drought and low food security, which is often exacerbated by climate change. So the already difficult situation is compounded by this violent extremism, organised crime, internal conflicts, and what together forms this breeding ground for the threat that is against us; terrorist movements, irregular migration. In other words, development has serious consequences, and if you look at the assessments, it is going in the wrong direction at the moment, and that is why we need to take action now, both from Denmark and Europe and from the rest of the world. We can and must help to make a positive difference. We can't be passive in this situation. The central issue for the government, as I said, is that civilian and military efforts go hand in hand. Humanitarian and development cooperation, regional stabilisation efforts and, yes, what we are also discussing today, military contributions to the MINUSMA in Mali and Operation Barkhane, this anti-terrorist operation that France is running. With the government's proposals, we will therefore build, as I said, on the good experience in the region, which several rapporteurs also mentioned. In the past, we have contributed to the UN mission in MINUSMA, for example. also to the forerunner of Operation Barkhane. The Danish contributions, which are covered by these two motions for resolutions, are all very much in demand. Transport capacities are one of the major challenges in this very unwise region. We must help to reduce fragility, prevent conflicts and also strengthen the ability of Sahel countries to safeguard their own security in order to combat terrorism, radicalisation and organised crime. If you look at those countries, you can see that there are also many places where there is no functioning state apparatus. One of the reasons why terrorist organisations are gaining ground is that the state does not provide security, but does not provide basic necessities to the population either. So I would like once again to thank all the parties, all the rapporteurs for one, I think, very constructive debate and for the broad support for these two motions for resolutions that have been made today. This shows that we are still willing to deliver a broad-spectrum effort for the benefit of the Sahel region. I also think it is very important when we send our soldiers, women and men out into the world that there is broad and good support here in the Folketing. So thank you for the floor.

Trump might have thought that he could just buy Greenland • Our master preserved, he has not yet figured out how a country works, so maybe it's time. At least he was told that he couldn't, and it was good, and I also know that our Greenlandic friends would say: couldn't you just go home to the United States? We'll take care of ourselves. I guess that's the way it is. But the Chinese are also promoting business, and why are they doing so? It may be because they are interested in getting close to the United States so that they might be able to control the United States. That is also one of the things we need to be aware of: why do the various great powers have an interest in Greenland? They do, because it is very strategic in the Arctic region, and it is, of course, a very interesting area, it is clear. There are many signs in the sun and moon that the world, also in addition to the United States and China, is showing interest in Greenland, and here we must then just stand together as a community of kingdoms, two countries woven together in a band, two people woven together by history and the thousands of families that make up the Danish-Greenland community. I would like to say that I come from North Jutland, where we have a lot of Greenlandic and Faroese friends and I know a great deal about Northern Jutland. So there are bonds that tie us together across it all. Therefore, it is also important that Denmark shows that in every way we want the Kingdom and support Greenlandic interests, because there must never be a contradiction between Greenlandic and Danish interests. Internally, we may disagree on a great deal, but externally we are a unity that the family can fight, so that it crashes, but still stands together when it comes to it. In the Danish People's Party we want much more focus on Greenland and the Arctic, even if this may mean that we have to reduce our commitment elsewhere in the world. Denmark must focus its foreign policy on neighbourhoods such as the Arctic and the Baltic Sea, but drosle the efforts in the Middle East down. The idea also escapes with statements by former Ambassador Peter Taksøe-Jensen, who examined Danish foreign policy for the former government. That is why we in the Danish People's Party believe that a much greater Danish military effort is needed, especially with regard to Greenland, and in this connection I would say that I would be delighted if we also saw that Greenlandic soldiers were included in the forces that were present. Why should they not be able to make an effort? I am sure that there are many who really want to do so and that they will also help to make an effort for their own country. This is something that I think we should look at more in the Defence Committee. Of course, when they come to Denmark, they have the opportunity to serve as military service, but it is not the same as if you have your own little strength in Greenland. So it is certainly something that I think we should look at more, but of course it should only be if you in Greenland want to. We're not gonna pull anything down on you like that. We need an extra Challenger aircraft, which, because of its long-range flight capacity, is fantastic as a surveillance aircraft, and we need more F-16 flights, so no one can be in doubt that Denmark enforces sovereignty over the full territory of the Kingdom Community. The idea of setting up a Greenlandic Home Guard was what I was talking about just before, but also the fact that people are definitely part of the Danish military and can help to make an effort for Greenland, as I said, is something that we fully support. But it's not just about military. Some have talked about intensifying research around Greenland with a new marine research ship, and we are certainly not opposed to that. The question then is what kind of ship should be up there. One might look at: Do we have any ships that are already there that we can use, or is it a brand new ship that needs to be built because there are special conditions in Greenland that we need to be aware of? But it is also important that Denmark engages in the Arctic Council. The world's great powers will enter the Arctic and exploit the opportunities to earn money, but not all have the necessary understanding of nature and people in the Arctic, and therefore it is very important that the kingdom has a strong voice in Arctic cooperation and that our work to develop the Arctic is sustainable and benefits the people who live up there. This can happen through the Arctic Council, where it is still a riddle for the Danish People's Party, how China can play a role. They have nothing to do with the Arctic, but perhaps the Minister for Foreign Affairs can explain a little about why. The ice melts in the summer, and the ice sea is largely free of ice; it opens up new opportunities: there are opportunities for new fishing, it opens up new shipping routes and there is a growing interest in tourism in the Arctic. Fortunately, the Arctic coastal states have now agreed and signed an agreement that fishing should not be carried out in the Arctic Sea until we are sure of the level of fish stocks and before we are sure that fishing is sustainable at all. Basically, a great deal needs to be done in terms of the environment and natural areas and in terms of protecting the Arctic nature. The Arctic is a large and resourceful area, and as one of the five major actors in the Arctic, we therefore have a great responsibility to ensure that people and nature can go hand in hand. We must ensure that those who wish the Arctic well are given the opportunity to work in a region, but also have a responsibility to ensure that development benefits those who live there. We have an obligation to safeguard the interests of the Kingdom in the Arctic, whether it be fishing, the environment, raw materials or tourism, and we have an obligation to ensure order in the Arctic. We must ensure that those who want the good and with the necessary respect for nature have access to the Arctic, and this must be the point of reference for the Danish People's Party and the government.

So, we have just heard that there are plans to arm in Kangerlussuaq by plane, and we have also sent an extra frigate to Greenland. Fortunately, the frigate can do other good than just being a military tool. It can also work with the civilian stuff. But isn't it a little difficult to tell the others that we don't want armament in the area and that we're in it ourselves? Yeah, I'll ask you in the first place. I think it looks strange when we want what we're talking about. It can easily be a talk, and then we do the opposite of what we say. There is something third that is important when we say that the climate is the most important and that low-voltage is just as important in the wake of it, namely that it is the inhabitants of the region and their interests that we look at first when we have to do things up there. In my opinion, they must have optimized their influence. Denmark is isolated from no Arctic country, but it is through our cooperation with Greenland that we have anything at all to have said in the Arctic. That is why it is important that we say that the Greenlandic voice and, for that matter, also like the Faroese voice on behalf of the Kingdom Community must sound wherever it is possible, because it is they who know where the shoe is pressing, and it is they who know how to move forward. Then I would like to say a few words about the new Arctic strategy. There I am disappointed, not that it has not come, because it is good enough to be thorough, but why is it only working at official level? I saw that an Arctic dialogue meeting was held from all over the Kingdom community at official level. When we did the world goals at the United Nations, it was a success because politicians and officials were pushed aside a little and NGOs were allowed to work around the world with these goals. Why don't we? Why are we not saying that we have bigger visions than officials and politicians can perform in this context and then involve all the peoples around the Arctic? I think that would be visionary, especially if it is the NGOs in the Kingdom community that sit down and are invited to a dialogue meeting. I would like to call on the Minister to try to work with us for a little while longer, because I think that is where the real innovation comes from. All respect for the officials no problem with it; they have their forces, but they also have their weaknesses. They don't live in that world every day. So I would like to say, when we start the strategy, that my immediate proposals will be: focus on the climate with all the ideas that may lie out with the grass roots; continue low-voltage, where we go ahead ourselves; no more foreign bases in our area; nor a deep port for the great American ship that Trump dreams of having space for; nor more military aircraft at airports, if we can avoid it. One could also consider extending the Arctic Council to discuss security policy. It sounds grotesque, but for example. I would like it to be the Arctic Council that is discussing arms control in the area. That would be the best place to discuss it. Then I would like to say that it is important that we try to continue to expand research. This could be much, much more central to the new strategy. But I would really, really like to hear the grassroots give their specific offers, and then I think we will have a strategy which is very much above what we have today and which can really provide the answers to what we need in the future in the Arctic. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20191009200218	Thank you for your statement. The time has elapsed since Danish politicians decide on Greenland's foreign policy. The words are the PM's. Inuit Ataqatigiit agrees with the recital. The need to strengthen Greenland's foreign policy role is both urgent and necessary. President Trump recently gave Greenland much needed attention, not only abroad, but also here in Denmark, and for many it was an abrupt awakening that we should not take our kingdom community for granted. More than ever before, there has been a need to fight for a more equal partnership, not by dwelling on the past, but to a great extent by looking forward. And that's why Inuit Ataqatigiit will also talk about our visions for the Arctic 2030. Therefore, the speech can also be seen as an input in relation to a future Arctic strategy, which is based on the UN's sustainability objectives. On the Inuit Ataqatigiits side, we would like to point out some concrete solutions that can lift the fine words about community and partnership to concrete actions. First of all, in the past we have also supported Greenland's own Arctic strategy and that it is done in coordination with Denmark. The government handled Trump's offer to buy Greenland very well. Together with Greenland, you stood shoulder to shoulder. Naalaksuisut declared Greenland open for trade, but not for sale. And the whole incident, I don't think we've heard the last of it. Soft power and an increased charm offensive on the American side we have to deal with together. At the same time, Greenland must also be given room for manoeuvre and the possibility of conducting foreign policy on its own in repatriated areas, as stated in the Self-Government Act. All of this also presupposes that we expand our privacy space and that we find some practical solutions for sharing confidential material and that we increase the meeting frequency. It may sound extremely low-practical, but it is of great value to both parties. Of other solutions, we would also like to point out the need for an Arctic minister, an Arctic ambassador who actually comes from the Arctic, and permanent places in the Foreign Policy Name, so that Greenland and Denmark always have knowledge of their own country. Finally, we also believe that there is a need for a debate on an Arctic Security Forum and how we can ensure it in the long term. Developments in the Arctic and the increased awareness of Greenland also call for fact-based foreign policy locally anchored in Greenland. And the decisions that are being made today will have a major impact on how Greenland, the Kingdom and the Arctic will develop in the coming decades. The balancing of advantages and disadvantages makes complex demands on politicians, officials and the general population of Greenland. Knowledge is power and knowledge is sought. But the supply in the Arctic is limited. Perhaps it is time for Arctic research to actually take place in the Arctic. From Inuit Ataqatigiits side, we have therefore pointed out in the ongoing financial negotiations the establishment of a knowledge centre for foreign and security policy at Ilisimatusarfik, the University of Greenland, also known as GIIAS, the Greenland Institute for International and Arctic Studies, a centre which we believe can help to be a local knowledge base that can strengthen Greenland's foreign and security policy position. It is agreed that Greenland's economy must be strengthened. An increase in exports to the United States, Asia and other major markets also requires a greater focus, both in Greenland, but also here in the Danish Parliament, on several free trade agreements, on increased export promotions and on more investments in Greenland. A strengthened economic base and many more young people in education completing their education are at the heart of a bright Greenlandic future. We need money for growth, you can popularly say, and preferably sustainable growth, which not only robs our many good natural resources in Greenland here and now, but one that points towards stronger diversified business development in Greenland. Due to the economic growth in the Arctic, the investment bank Guggenheim Partners estimates that in the next 15 years 1 billion is needed. Dollar in investment in infrastructure in the area. This is in many ways a very strong picture of the potential for development that we face in Greenland. But potential doesn't do it alone. History has shown that to us too. Action is needed and hands are needed. For there is a great deal of work going on in Greenland, and in Nuuk alone there are currently some 700 skilled workers, especially the construction industry, and with the expansion of the new airports around Greenland, demand for craftsmen will increase further significantly. Business is screaming for labour that we cannot deliver on Greenland alone. From the Inuit Ataqatigiits side, we in the Danish Parliament would like to help push for the establishment of a fixed track system which knows it from the Faroe Islands and which can ensure a smooth residence and work permit process in Greenland. If we do not act now, we will shortly see a slowing down in the development and growth of Greenland, and that is actually something that we in the Folketing and the government can and must do something about. Together, we also need to be better at marketing the Arctic as a place for value to run business and invest in. Together we have a large number of obvious platforms that are not well enough exploited today. We see, for example, how Danish embassies around the world help to use the Greenland flag. It could also be an opportunity to ensure a massive information effort around the world, of course in cooperation with Greenland. We would like to see the embassies also help to improve support for Greenland's export promotion and help attract more tourists and investments to Greenland. It is obvious to earmark funds from the Danish Export Council, The Trade Council, for projects for Greenlandic companies. One might also ask oneself the question: Perhaps we should work together to further develop Kangerlussuaq as a data hub, as an Arctic research centre. It will be obvious to take advantage of the cold and the space that we have around Greenland. We have a huge land area that could be a test country in terms of sustainable solutions in an Arctic environment. In other words, these could also be solutions that we could export to other Arctic countries, because together we are helping to develop something new. Last but not least, when we talk about business development, there is a huge untapped potential in relation to the new shipping routes in the Arctic. The ice melts, and you can say that Greenland is becoming greener, and that creates some opportunities, of course, but it also creates really big challenges in the area. The northwest passage becomes more accessible, and here it is obvious to see whether Nuuk's new, modern port, Sikuki, can be used as a shipping hub in relation to reloading ships coming from the passage and moving on into the world. We are very pleased that the Arctic is being highlighted in the changing governments' foreign and security policy strategies. Unfortunately, it sounds a little hollow when the government on the strategy paper prioritises the Arctic, but does not allocate sufficient funds for real work. And remember, there are 4 million people living in the Arctic. And the important work to ensure the rights of indigenous peoples in the area is something that we should work together on more. That is why we also believe that money should be allocated to the ICC and IWGIA and their work in the Arctic Council with indigenous peoples. The Kingdom is to return to the lead as a champion of indigenous peoples. A new study from Greenland Perspective shows that as much as 63 per cent. considers that Greenland should accede to the Paris Agreement. So does Inuit Ataqatigiit. Today we get more than 70%. of our current in Greenland from renewable sources, and the goal is that we should reach 100 per cent. And Denmark is full of good experiences in green conversion, which is why we also believe that the fund and the initiatives taken from the Danish pension sector to invest in green conversion should also deal with green conversion in the Arctic. If we want to be a Green Kingdom community and have green cooperation, then we could expand the expertise that there is so that we can find new green solutions in the Arctic. Inuit Ataqatigiit thanks for the floor and looks forward to the debate. We would urge that work be based in the near future on trust, respect and maximum involvement. And then we hope that our input can feed into a good debate about a meaningful kingdom community up to 2030. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-DK_20191009204324	Thank you. First of all, I would like to thank you for a very good, objective and constructive debate. As a kingdom, we have core interests at stake, and I think, as far as the statement is concerned, which, of course, is looking back, because it describes what has been going on over the past year and where many rapporteurs were concerned about looking forward, that the debate on the forward-looking that we are going to get going is even more important. It can of course be said that the statement makes clear the importance of international cooperation taking place in the Arctic and, above all, the total commitment of the Kingdom across the kingdoms with the many authorities that have activities in the Arctic, and let me briefly mention the work on the Kingdom's new common Arctic strategy, for it was mentioned by several rapporteurs. After all, the strategy we have is from 2011 and it will formally expire at the end of 2020, i.e. by the end of next year. The strategy has provided the three Kingdom parts with a platform, a framework, for the international Arctic work. It has given the Kingdom impact and visibility, not least in the Arctic Council, and I think that is important to hold on to that. I also see the work on developing a new strategy as one of the most important things we are going to do. It is true that the work has started at the level of civil servants. I have only been in my duties for a few months, and I look forward to entering the work politically as well. I know that it is in full swing at official level both in Denmark, Greenland and the Faroe Islands, and of course all relevant stakeholders and stakeholders will be consulted regularly, including in Denmark, i.e. the Committee on Greenland and Faroe Islands and the Parliament's Arctic Delegation, which was also mentioned, and I look forward to the dialogue with you and colleagues in Greenland and the Faroe Islands on the strategy in the coming months. I think it is very important that we have a proper and broad dialogue, as several speakers have said. I also agree with many of the comments made by the House today. The discussion has, of course, in some way been very much about the significant developments taking place in the Arctic as a result of geopolitical developments and the consequences it has for the global security policy picture. We must remember that the developments we have seen in the Arctic over recent years are due to many things. It is due, among other things, to: climate change, as several rapporteurs mentioned, the melting of ice, new trade routes, the North-East Passage. This presents difficult challenges both globally and in the Arctic, but it also leads to new economic opportunities that we must understand to deal with properly. At the same time, developments have also clearly shown that the Arctic region has an increasing geopolitical impact on the strategic balance between the United States, Russia and China. This can also be seen in the form of e.g.: the military construction that Russia has in the Arctic, it is reflected in the Chinese Arctic Strategy from 2018, as some know, where China describes itself as a near-Arctic state, although in reality they are quite far from the Arctic, and most recently the United States has recently prioritised the Arctic, which is increasingly seen through a security policy prism. So it can be said that geopolitics has, as it were, moved into the Arctic region. It has long been a matter of course to speak of the Arctic as a region of peace and low tension. This is no longer something we can take for granted, but one can say that the objective of low-voltage is increasingly challenged. I would say that the Kingdom, as an Arctic coastal state and a member of the Arctic Council, has an important role in influencing development, we must be proactive, as was also mentioned here, and there is also, in general, a discussion about the need for an architecture of Arctic cooperation. We already have that today, namely the Arctic Council with the five Arctic coastal states and eight members of the Arctic Council, and I think it is important to make this cooperation even more visible and anchored. This also means that when other countries outside the Arctic region are interested in the Arctic and want to be observers, etc., we must take a positive view of this from the outset, because it helps to maintain and ensure that the architecture we have, which is based on international law and law in the Arctic area, is something that we hold very firmly to on the part of the Kingdom. After all, our aim is to create a prosperous and sustainable future for the Arctic. We must exploit the region and the new economic opportunities in a sustainable way. This must be done primarily for the benefit of the inhabitants of the Arctic. We must also work to ensure that, as I said, Arctic cooperation remains firmly anchored in international rule-based cooperation. I would just like to say here that, when there is pressure on international standards and rules, when there is an increase in superpower rivalry, as we see it, which also somehow has a spill-over effect in the Arctic area, I think it is very important that in the Kingdom, in the Kingdom community, we keep very, very close together on these principles, and I also agree that, although we sometimes have different governments in the three kingdoms, we have a fundamental common interest to stand together in the world we are looking into, and we must of course also show that with the strategy we need to develop, how we do in practice. The Arctic Council is the main forum for discussion of issues and is based on a wide range of areas: sustainable economic development, people's living conditions, environment, climate, research, sea rescue, search, maritime waste, etc., and this is important. I would also like to say that, of course, it was extremely worrying when, for the first time in the history of the Council, no agreement was reached on a ministerial declaration, mainly because of climate issues. Here we can also say that there was a clear disagreement between most Arctic states and a single state about how important this issue is. Then we managed to make a ministerial statement that signals the outside world that we continue to solve the challenge through dialogue and cooperation. But here we have an example of where among other things. The Kingdom of Denmark and the United States disagree on how important the climate challenge actually is. It can be said that, despite the fact that security policy has been specifically excluded from the mandate of the Arctic Council, cooperation in itself helps to support the objective of low-voltage by providing the Arctic States with this common framework. That is why I am so concerned that the common framework will be strengthened, anchored and visible in the years to come. A good and close cooperation with all three kingdoms is therefore the key to the effective promotion of the Kingdom's Arctic priorities, and therefore an Arctic dialogue meeting will be held in Tórshavn in November, as far as I am aware. This is the third time in three years that officials meet and discuss some of these priorities. I would also like to make it clear that it is a huge priority for me and for the government that we are working to strengthen efforts in the Arctic and cooperation within the Kingdom Community. I myself am going to Reykjavík tomorrow to attend Arctic Circle, which is one of the important conferences, and discuss these issues with a wide range of key players and representatives from all three parts of the Kingdom community, and I think it is important that we are here. Bent Bøgsted, Sjúrður Skaale, Karsten Hønge and others are asking about Danish marine research and also about the marine research ship Dana I am, in fact, very aware that there is this demand that after 2023 Denmark also has access to a marine research ship to support work with, among other things. fishing stocks and research in Arctic waters. After all, the existing ship is owned by DTU, which receives payment from, for example, the Ministry of the Environment and Defence for renting the ship. A new ship is, after all, a major investment; it is something that must be given priority in the context of the other investments in the field of research, and I would also like to point out that this discussion continues with the Minister of Education and Research. As several speakers have said, we have also had a debate on whether there is an increasing militarisation of the Arctic area and how we react to it. I think, sir. Karsten Hønge mentioned that it was important that, on the one hand, we can enforce our sovereignty and secure our borders in the Kingdom and, on the other hand, by no means unnecessarily contribute to anything that does away with this low-voltage, which is our focus, and I think that is very right to bear that in mind. But there is an increasing interest, as I mentioned, and with greater accessibility, the need for monitoring, for the enforcement of sovereignty and for rescue services in this vast area is also increasing. The harsh climate means that there may be a need for military capabilities to solve civilian tasks. Christian Juhl said that it could be important and, among other things, it could be important. That is why we and the other Arctic states are, after all, strengthening our military prints in the area. But having said that, the defense intelligence service has among other things. pointing out that Russia's military construction in the region is increasingly containing offensive elements. This applies not least to the preparations to be able to station fighters, air-tanking aircraft on a base in the northern Arctic Ocean. The base is located approx. 1,000 km from Greenland, and the distance means that Russian fighter planes will be able to reach the airspace over north-east Greenland at short notice, and with the air tank capacity they could reach Thule Base. This is an example of the issues that we obviously have to deal with together with our allies, but it does not change our objective of low-voltage that there are these things. Denmark strongly supports research in the Arctic, we want to promote research activities. That's why the kingdom, among others, has... The European Union and its Member States welcome the signing of the agreement on the promotion of research in the Arctic States. In this particular case, to which reference is made, in relation to a research expedition we have had to deal with a number of issues from the Danish side which do not include the research project itself, but which mean that it has not been possible to fully comply with the application. Having said that, this is a research project which the government would like to see carried out and we will therefore reach out to the Swiss Polar Institute SPI to discuss how the research project can take place, possibly at a later stage. Because that is something that we give a very high priority to. The question is also raised of how in practice we involve both the Faroe Islands and Greenland in foreign policy matters, including the establishment of secure communication, and this is also a priority area for me as Foreign Minister. It is important that we can share information, that we have an equal standing in the Kingdom Community, that we can see the same images at the same time and thus relate to the world around us. So that is one of the things I am working on and will be taking initiatives for us to do in practice as well, because I think that is very important. There are also questions about the service contract at Thule. This is a matter which the government takes very seriously. It is absolutely crucial that we find a solution that ensures that Greenland will benefit as much as possible from the American military presence. Denmark and Greenland have a good and close contact on the matter. Together, we are very aware of the great American interest in Greenland and whether it can contribute to a sensible solution. In any case, however, we must continue to be prepared for this to take time. This is a complex matter and, as I said, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs is in close contact with the Department of Foreign Affairs in Nuuk and relevant actors in Washington, and I cannot go into such specific details for the sake of the negotiations, but I can say that the matter should not be closed at all costs. It is also about Greenland getting the greatest benefit from the American presence. There are many other issues that have been raised, among others. China's role in relation to the Arctic. I think, sir. Peter Seier Christensen and also Mr. Sjúrður Skaale mentioned this in their speech, and yes, we welcome the fact that China has an active involvement in the region. Both Greenland and Denmark can benefit very well from the cooperation with China in the Arctic, both in relation to the Arctic Council and in relation to the potential commercial activities, but of course we should not be naive. It can be said that China is thinking long-term strategically about its investments and presence, for example. through the Silk Road initiative, and therefore geopolitical considerations also play a huge role. We're not gonna get out of this. If you take the public risk assessment of FE, i.e. Defence Intelligence, it is estimated that the Chinese military has an increased interest in the Arctic and strengthens its knowledge of the region. But it is also estimated that so far there has been a very limited Chinese military activity in the Arctic. In the same way, Denmark, Greenland and the Faroe Islands must think long-term and together consider how we get the most out of this development, but at the same time also take the necessary precautions. This applies, I think, to security policy in particular, of course. We need to balance, to a greater extent than in the past, various considerations in the foreign and security policy of the empire: the concern for our trade and the need for investment and development, but also our natural concern for security in all parts of the empire. We are fully aware of this. However, I would like to conclude by thanking the rapporteurs and looking forward to continuing the debate. I do not see this as the end of a debate on an Arctic statement, but as the beginning of how we can together put forward a good strategy from 2021 onwards. This is something that I personally would like to throw myself on a lot. I think it's insanely important that all parts of the community take this on themselves. That is also why I am going to Iceland tomorrow and intend to go to Greenland and the Faroe Islands very soon, for example. to discuss these things that we have also discussed here today. So thank you for the floor.
